model, questions per student per day, longest answer. Saving asks for a
reason and writes a SECURITY audit row, like the governance kill switches,
because turning it on starts sending students' draft code to a third party.
Switching it off takes effect on the very next request.

**What leaves the college, every time a student asks**: the public problem
statement, that student's own draft code, the verdict of their own last
practice submission, and the sample case it failed — the one the product
already shows them. Never hidden test data, never another student's work,
never anything that says who the student is. One module makes that call
(`apps/web/src/lib/ai/provider.ts`) and `tests/repo/ai-helper-posture.test.ts`
fails the build if a second one appears.

**What it costs a student.** Asking makes a later *first* solve ASSISTED:
it still counts as solved and still pays XP at the assisted rate, but it is
excluded from the unassisted evidence a readiness score is built on — the
same rule a tier-3 hint and the editorial already follow. Without it every
readiness number in the college would quietly inflate. The button unlocks
only after one judged submission or ten minutes of work on that problem.

**If students say it is broken.** `/super/settings/ai` shows answered and
failed counts for the last 30 days. A failure is recorded with no answer
and costs the student no quota. The page also says, in a banner, when the
deployment cannot answer at all — no key, or a college-hosted model with no
`AI_BASE_URL`. While that is true the Assistant tab does not render, so
students see no button rather than a button that fails.

**To stop it immediately**: `/super/settings/ai` → **Off** → save. No deploy,
no restart; the setting is read per request. Removing the key does the same
thing and needs a restart.

---

### 2.17 Running a mock interview (Phase 12h)

A mentor offers an hour; a student takes it; at the time both open the same
room and the mentor watches the student code. There is no switch — the
feature is on, and the only thing an operator does is understand the clock.

**The mentor's side** is `/mentor/mock-interviews`. "Offer a slot" takes a
start time (IST, as every admin form does) and a length between 15 and 120
minutes. The slot appears to every student until somebody takes it; until
then the mentor can call it off with a reason.

**The student's side** is `/mock-interviews`, under ASSESS. Open slots are
listed with the mentor's name and the time. A student holds at most **two**
booked slots at once — the refusal says so, and the fix is to sit one or
hand one back.

**The clock, which is where every support question comes from:**

| When | What is possible |
|---|---|
| until 60 minutes before | the student may hand the slot back; it becomes open again |
| from 60 to 10 minutes before | neither: the student is committed. The way out is to call it off with a reason, which the mentor reads |
| from 10 minutes before to the end | both sides can open the room; the mentor presses Start |
| from 10 minutes after the start | the mentor may mark a no-show (not before — a slow lift is not a no-show on somebody's record) |
| after the end | the room is closed. An interview left running is finished by the mentor writing it up |

**What the mentor sees while it runs** is the student's editor, updating a
few seconds behind, and nothing else — no camera, no microphone, no screen.
Both sides are expected to be on a call of their own choosing; the platform
does not place one and records nothing. The mirror is **read-only**: there
is no way for an interviewer to type into a candidate's editor, and that is
deliberate (`docs/OPEN-QUESTIONS.md`, Phase 12h).

**Finishing** asks for three ratings and a written note. It goes to the
student and counts for nothing else: no XP, no leaderboard, no readiness
input, no band. If a mentor asks what the ratings "do", the answer is that
the student reads them.

**If the mirror says "Reconnecting…"** and stays there: the stream is an
`EventSource` on `/api/mock/[interviewId]/live`, which closes itself when
the interview is no longer running and after ten minutes in any case (the
page reopens it). A mentor who is not that interview's mentor gets nothing
from it, by design — check who offered the slot before looking further.

---

### 2.18 Written and system-design questions (Phase 12i)

A TA writes a question and the rubric it is marked against; students
answer in prose; a TA marks it. There is no switch -- the feature is on,
and nothing about it runs code.

**Writing one** is `/mentor/written`. The form takes a kind (system
design, or a written answer), a title, the question in markdown, roughly
how long it should take, and **two to eight criteria** with a weight of
1-5 each. It saves as a **draft**: nobody can see it or answer it until
"Publish it".

**The rubric is published with the question and frozen when it opens.**
If a criterion is wrong, retire the question and write another one -- a
student marked 12 of 16 against four criteria did not get that score
against five, and the database refuses the alternative.

**Retiring is not deleting.** A retired question leaves every student's
list except that of somebody who answered it, and their answer and its
mark stay readable. A question's author may retire it, and so may an
ADMIN or SUPER_ADMIN -- a TA who leaves must not take a published
question with them.

**Marking** is the queue on `/mentor/written`, shared between everybody
who can mark and ordered oldest-first. Open a row and the page shows the
question, the answer and the rubric together. Every criterion starts
*unmarked*: a default would be a mark nobody made, and the save is
refused until all of them carry one. Two ways to finish:

| What | When to use it | What the student sees |
|---|---|---|
| Save the mark | there is something to say per criterion | the weighted total with its denominator, each criterion's mark and note, and the overall note |
| Hand it back unmarked | a mark would say less than a sentence | the sentence, and their own draft back to work on |

**What the mark does: nothing.** No XP, no leaderboard, no readiness, no
band, nothing a recruiter sees. If a TA asks what the percentage is for,
the answer is that the student reads it.

**The one thing to tell a student** who asks whether this is anonymous:
it is not. The person marking sees their name, the page says so above
the box, and that is deliberate -- an answer is coursework, unlike an
interview archive entry (Phase 12e), which is anonymous precisely
because every student reads it.

---

### 2.19 The SQL sandbox and its judge (Phase 12k)

SQL problems are judged by `apps/sql-judge`, a judge node with a
PostgreSQL sandbox instead of an isolate box. It is the only process in the
platform that holds a database credential other than the app and the
scheduler, and the credential is the sandbox's — never the application's.

**Starting it in development:**

```powershell
node scripts/dev/sql-sandbox-up.mjs   # creates recx_sql_sandbox + two roles
npx pnpm@9.15.9 --filter @recx/sql-judge dev
```

The script writes `apps/sql-judge/.env.local` (gitignored) with both
connection strings and generates the passwords; it is idempotent and keeps
the passwords it already wrote. In development the node id defaults to
`sql-dev-1`, and it is a node of its own: copy the `RECX_NODE_ID` and
`RECX_NODE_TOKEN` the script wrote into `apps/web/.env.local` as
`JUDGE_DEV_SQL_NODE_ID` / `JUDGE_DEV_SQL_NODE_TOKEN` and re-run the seed,
which registers both engines' nodes (§1.3). The script says so on its last
line when that pair is still unset.

Skip it and the failure is quiet in the worst way: the SQL judge boots,
passes its self-check, reports `sql judge ready` on its queues — and then
every heartbeat comes back `HTTP 401 — Judge node authentication failed.`
as a warning in its log, while a student's SQL submission sits at
`DISPATCHED` for ever with nothing on the page to say why. Running both
engines under one node id instead works, but only one at a time: the
`JudgeNode` row's `driver` column shows whichever process beat last, and the
two overwrite each other's advertised languages.

**What it does at boot, in order:**

| Step | What it means when it fails |
|---|---|
| refuses application secrets in its environment | somebody gave a judge host the app's `.env`; fix the unit file, not the check |
| checks the cluster | `REFUSING TO RUN` means the "sandbox" is the application's database. Not overridable |
| sweeps stale `job_…` schemas | a judge died mid-case. One schema is a leaked dataset; a growing count every boot means the host is being killed |
| runs the adversarial self-check | a grant somebody loosened by hand. The node keeps working and the fleet withholds the sensitive lanes |
| heartbeats as `driver: postgres` | the app host is down, or the node token is wrong |

**The two things that go wrong in the sandbox, and what they look like:**

* **A schema left behind.** `SELECT nspname FROM pg_namespace WHERE nspname
  LIKE 'job\_%'` as the owner. The next boot drops them; dropping them by
  hand is safe at any time, because no live job's schema outlives its case
  by more than a few milliseconds.
* **A student's query that will not die.** It cannot outlive
  `statement_timeout` (five seconds) — if one appears to, the connection is
  stuck rather than the query, and the judge closes it after three seconds
  and destroys the socket. A connection count that climbs without jobs
  running is the thing to look at (`SELECT count(*) FROM pg_stat_activity
  WHERE application_name = 'recx-sql-judge'`).

**Rotating the sandbox credentials:** re-run the script with the two URL
lines removed from `.env.local` (it generates new passwords and re-applies
them), then restart the judge. Nothing else in the platform holds them.

**What it must never have:** the application's `DATABASE_URL`, `AUTH_SECRET`,
`IP_HASH_PEPPER`, an object-store key, or a connection to the application's
cluster. The process refuses to start with any of them, and that refusal is
a feature rather than an inconvenience.

**Bringing one up in production** (`deploy/compose.sql-judge.yml`, never
run here — there is no such host):

```bash
# 1. the cluster, then the role whose whole definition is what it may NOT do
docker compose -f deploy/compose.sql-judge.yml --env-file deploy/sql-judge.env up -d sandbox-db
sudo scripts/ops/sql-sandbox-provision.sh

# 2. register the node on the app host and drop its token in place
#    (/run/recx/secrets/sql_judge_node_token), then
docker compose -f deploy/compose.sql-judge.yml --env-file deploy/sql-judge.env up -d
```

The cluster is on the judge host, not the database host, and is in no
backup: it holds per-submission schemas and nothing else, so losing it
costs a rerun. `synchronous_commit=off` and `full_page_writes=off` are set
for the same reason — a crash loses work that is seconds old and
reproducible by definition.

**Reading the fleet page with two engines** (`/admin/judge`):

| What you see | What it means |
|---|---|
| **Engines** table, a red row | work is waiting on that engine and no node of it is online. This is the state the `JudgeEngineBacklog` alert fires on |
| a node whose Engine says `postgres` | its **Judge / engine** row is the judge's version and PostgreSQL's, not isolate's |
| Calibration: "Not measured for the SQL engine" | not a gap in that node's reporting. The SQL engine has no hello-world benchmark, because a query's limit is the database's own `statement_timeout` |
| a SQL node that never serves ASSESSMENT or CONTEST | deliberate, and the restriction says so in words. Until there is a benchmark for the query engine, an exam is not judged on one |

The lane figures above the table are the SUM of both engines' queues. A
lane that looks busy with the Engines table showing one engine idle is one
engine's problem, not a capacity problem.

---

### 2.20 Running a hall on REC Exam Browser (Phase 14f)

The other kiosk option. `KIOSK_SEB` and `KIOSK_RECX` both exist and a paper
is set to one of them; this is the second. `docs/EXAM-BROWSER.md` is what
the application is and what it is worth — read §6 before telling anybody
what it guarantees, because the honest answer is narrower than it looks.

**What it is worth, in one line for an invigilator:** it raises the cost of
the easy ways to get help and leaves a record a person reads. It is not a
claim that nobody can cheat, and the phone on the desk is outside the reach
of any software. You are still the control that matters most.

#### Before the day

1. **Publish a build.** `deploy/exam-browser/README.md`. Check one download
   per platform from outside the college network; a 404 here is a hall that
   cannot sit.
2. **Turn the download on**, on `/super/settings/integrations` → REC Exam
   Browser → "Hand it out". It is off until somebody turns it on, and the
   switch governs the download itself, not just the button: turning it off
   refuses the file to anybody who kept the link. The screen warns you if
   the switch would publish nothing, which happens when
   `EXAM_BROWSER_DOWNLOAD_DIR` is unset on the deployment or the directory
   holds no build the manifest names.
3. **Tell candidates where it comes from.** `/exam-browser` is public, says
   what the application does and what it cannot do, and offers the
   installer for each platform. Point at that page, not at a file in a chat
   message: a candidate who learns to install exam software from a link
   somebody sent them has learnt the wrong thing, and that habit is the one
   this whole design is least able to protect them from.
4. **Have them install the day before.** Two hundred candidates downloading
   ninety megabytes at nine in the morning is the deployment serving
   eighteen gigabytes through the same processes that are running the
   exam.
5. **Set the paper to REC Exam Browser.** A published paper's proctor mode
   is fixed by a trigger, so this is chosen when the paper is created. A
   paper already published in another mode cannot be flipped — which is the
   rule working: a paper's meaning must not change under the candidates
   sitting it.
6. **Check `EXAM_BROWSER_SECRET` is set** in the deployment's environment
   (`/run/recx/secrets/exam_browser_secret`, wired in
   `deploy/compose.app.yml`).
   Every sitting key is derived from it. The application refuses to start in
   production with the development default, so a missing one is a hall that
   cannot sit rather than a hall sitting with a known key — but find out
   the week before, not at nine.

#### On the day

1. Candidates sign in **in their ordinary browser**, open the paper and
   reach the readiness check. The Launch panel is there instead of the
   usual checks.
2. Pressing **Launch** mints a single-use token, good for five minutes, and
   hands it to the operating system. The exam browser opens full screen on
   the readiness page, already signed in — nobody should be typing a
   password into a window with no address bar, and if anybody is, stop and
   read the next section.
3. They press **Start** inside that window and sit the paper.
4. The sitting ends when they hand in. The application quits and leaves
   nothing running.

#### When something goes wrong

| What the candidate sees | What it means | What to do |
|---|---|---|
| Nothing happens on Launch | The application is not installed, or the scheme is not registered | Install it. The readiness page's own status line says this; `/exam-browser` has the version. |
| "That launch has already been used" | The token was spent twice | Press Launch again — a fresh token costs nothing. **If they only pressed it once, tell someone:** two copies holding one sitting's key is the event worth seeing, and it is in the audit log as `exam.browser.launch_reused`. |
| "This launch pointed somewhere this exam browser is not allowed to go" | Something tried to point the college's application at a server it was not shipped to talk to | **Do not let them enter a password.** This is not the candidate's fault. Record the machine and tell whoever runs the platform. |
| "Your exam browser is too old" | The installed copy is below the version floor | Install the current build. If a whole hall sees it, the floor is wrong, not the hall — see below. |
| The download link does nothing, or 404s | The switch is off, or no build is published for that platform | `/super/settings/integrations` says which. Both are the same 404 to a candidate on purpose; the page above the button says which in words. |
| A sign-in form inside the exam browser | The sitting's session is gone: expired, revoked, or the paper was already handed in | Close it and launch again from the readiness check. A candidate should never type a password into that window. |
| The window shows an extra-screen warning | A second display is attached | Ask them to unplug it before the clock starts. It is reported, never refused: `docs/PROCTORING.md` §3 governs it like every other signal. |

#### Cutting off a bad build

`/super/settings/integrations` → **REC Exam Browser** → lowest accepted
version. SUPER_ADMIN only, audited at SECURITY with the previous value and
the new one.

Raising it refuses every candidate on an older copy **from their next
request onwards, including candidates part-way through a paper.** So:

* raise it when a build is known to be bad, not as housekeeping;
* never during a sitting, unless the sitting is the reason;
* if a hall cannot start and the floor was changed recently, clear it
  first and ask questions afterwards. The button is next to the field.

An unreadable stored floor is ignored and logged rather than applied, so a
typo cannot lock a hall out — but it will be silently doing nothing, which
is why it is logged.

#### What is not true of it

Written here because a runbook is where somebody looks for permission to
claim something:

* It does not watch the candidate. No camera, no microphone, no screen
  recording, no keystrokes, no reading of the clipboard. `/exam-browser`
  says so to candidates and `tests/repo/exam-browser-posture.test.ts` fails
  the build if any of it appears.
* It does not stop copy and paste. Paste is instrumented, not blocked
  (`docs/PROCTORING.md` §2), and a tool that types the clipboard as
  keystrokes walks past any block.
* It does not survive the sitting. No service, no scheduled task, no
  autostart entry, nothing in the system tray.
* **It does not make other applications unreachable.** The window holds
  the front of the screen, cannot be minimised, and swallows the shortcuts
  that open a second window, print, save or view source — so leaving the
  paper is deliberate rather than accidental, and nothing lands on top of
  it by itself. Alt-tab, the Windows key and the taskbar still belong to
  the operating system, and the candidate still owns the machine. If a
  hall genuinely needs other applications to be unreachable, that is a
  `LAB_SUPERVISED` sitting on college machines with `deploy/lab-policy/`
  applied, and §2.14 is how.
* It cannot see a second machine, a phone, or a person in the room. Those
  are yours.
* A `KIOSK_RECX` result is still not a credential. Only `LAB_SUPERVISED`
  results may feed shortlisting, certificates or placement eligibility
  (`docs/PROCTORING.md` §3).

---

The three cases that matter are asserted as DISTINCT outcomes rather than booleans: an **empty** allowlist is no restriction (not a lockout — a hall whose allowlist nobody filled in must not refuse the whole cohort), an allowlist of only malformed entries is treated as empty and reported as invalid so an admin is told, and a configured allowlist against an address we cannot parse **refuses**. IPv6 is parsed properly — `::ffff:10.0.0.1` round-trips to the same bytes as `::ffff:a00:1` and `2001:db8:::1` is rejected — and a block **never matches across address families**, so an IPv4 allowlist entry cannot silently admit a dual-stack client | PASS | Claude |
| 6d | — | A Postgres CHECK can be created and still be an invalid regex | Found 2026-10-19: `col ~ '^[A-Za-z0-9_-]{16,1024}$'` exceeds Postgres's repetition limit of 255. The constraint was created happily — both catalog fences reported it present and correct — and then raised `invalid regular expression: invalid repetition count(s)` on **every insert**, because Postgres compiles the pattern lazily. A guard that exists is not a guard that works; only the invariant test proved it. Split into `~ '^[A-Za-z0-9_-]+$' AND length(col) BETWEEN 16 AND 1024`. `docs/CONVENTIONS.md` | — |

The chain the platform then ran, in order: the worker died with its isolate boxes still allocated → the restarted worker's `isolate --init` failed on them ("This box is currently in use by another process") → each failure released the lease instead of posting a verdict → after **4 consecutive releases** the app **auto-drained the node** (audited `judge.node.auto_drain`, which is what that mechanism exists for) → the reconciler kept re-enqueueing the waiting jobs (`jobs.replaced` 18–22 every tick) while the node took nothing → `isolate --cleanup` on the job boxes, **Return to ONLINE** through `/admin/judge` with a reason (audited `judge.node.returned`) → every remaining submission judged. **Nothing was lost**: all 36 rows reached a terminal state (AC 27, CE 6, IE 3) with attempt counts 1×20, 2×7, 3×9. | 10f | 2026-10-20 | **A bug the drill found: six students' correct programs were recorded as CE** | **FIXED.** The six CEs in the row above all read `FileNotFoundError: [Errno 2] No such file or directory: 'main.py'`. A stale box is not a usable box, so the compile step ran against an empty one, the interpreter could not find the source, and the worker classified a non-zero compile exit as a **student's compile error**. A wrong verdict shown to a student is the one failure this platform must not have. `compileProgram` now checks, on a compile failure, whether the source file is actually in the box: if it is not, the box is broken and it is an `InfraError` — the lease is released, the job is retried on a healthy node, and three consecutive releases drain this one. `listFiles` failing counts as "present", so a transient listing error can never swallow a student's real compile error. Two tests in `apps/judge-worker/test/run-job.test.ts`: no source → InfraError, source present → still CE | Claude |

This is RUNBOOK §2.1's rule demonstrated rather than asserted: Postgres is truth, Redis is transport. | dev | 2026-10-21 | A sign-in that refused every attempt on the development machine — and what it was not | **FIXED, and nothing was wrong with the product.** `RECADMIN01` was answered with "Something went wrong. Please try again — if it keeps happening, quote reference …" on every attempt. That page is `POST /api/auth/login`'s answer to ANY unexpected exception (`?error=unavailable`, §1.8): the code is deliberately the same for a database outage as for a bug, and the reference is printed beside the exception in the web log, which is where this one was — Prisma `P1001 Can't reach database server at localhost:5433`. Two independent causes, both on the Windows↔WSL2 boundary. **One:** Windows resolves `localhost` to `::1` first and WSL2 serves only the IPv4 loopback, so the connection waited out a timeout on `::1` while `127.0.0.1:5433` connected on the first try — measured 20/20 against `127.0.0.1`, `ETIMEDOUT` against `[::1]`, with Redis unaffected because `REDIS_URL` already said `127.0.0.1`. **Two:** WSL stops the distribution within seconds of its last session exiting (16 s, measured), taking PostgreSQL, Redis and isolate with it — and `wsl -d Ubuntu -- pg_isready`, the obvious way to check, *starts* the distribution in order to run and so always answers happily. Fixes: `127.0.0.1` in both `.env.example` files, the RUNBOOK's own snippets and the 37 files holding a `RECX_TEST_DATABASE_URL` fallback; mirrored networking actually set in `%UserProfile%\.wslconfig`; a new `scripts/dev/stack-up.ps1` that holds the distribution open, starts the three services and proves from **Windows** that 5433 and 6379 answer; and `tests/repo/dev-stack.test.ts` so the examples cannot drift back. Verified: `/api/health` `{"ok":true,"db":"up","redis":"up","migrations":"current"}`, six consecutive admin sign-ins landing on `/admin`, a student sign-in landing on `/dashboard`, and the holder released and re-taken with the stack coming back both times | Claude |

The general form, which is the part worth keeping: **what the candidate's browser reports about the candidate's browser is not a control**, and a gate built on a self-reported value is strictly worse than a signal — when it is wrong it stops an honest student from sitting the paper instead of adding a line a reviewer can discount, and it chooses who on exam morning at random. So nothing was added to the exam client. What shipped instead is the control §4 lists first, which until now was a sentence: `deploy/lab-policy/` — `chrome-policy.json` (Linux/ChromeOS), `recx-exam-policy.reg` (Windows) and a README explaining every key — plus `docs/RUNBOOK.md` §2.14 with the install, the root-ownership check and a five-step per-hall verification at `chrome://policy`. `ExtensionInstallBlocklist: ["*"]` is the real "no extensions", and it holds because the college owns the policy file. Two findings worth the row: **Chrome Sync is the hole that would have undone it** — without `BrowserSignin: 0` and `SyncDisabled: true`, one Google sign-in reinstalls the candidate's own extension set on arrival and the blocklist is theatre; and the policy **disables** private mode rather than forcing it, because what the idea actually wants is a clean browser for the next candidate, which `ClearBrowsingDataOnExitList` does without private mode's own hazard (a window closed by accident takes the session with it, mid-sitting, against a running clock — the hazard the `/assessments/[slug]/readiness` page now warns the candidate about in one line). | 12c | 2026-10-30 | Spaced revision: the problems you solved, coming back | **DONE.** SPEC §11 calls this the highest-value thing on the post-launch list, "because the documented failure mode of sheet prep is solving 300 problems once and retaining none." A `RevisionCard` per student per problem, written by the progress engine on a first solve and moved by it afterwards: five rungs at 1, 3, 7, 21 and 60 days; a due re-solve climbs one and pays 25 % of the first solve (`XpEventKind.REVISION`, which existed in the enum since Phase 3 and had never been issued), a due attempt that fails drops one and stays due today, and clearing the top rung retires the card. Three decisions carry it. **The ladder moves only when the ledger did** — `applyRevision` advances the card after `insertXpEvent` reports an insert, so a retried finalise can neither pay twice nor climb twice. **Three a day is a property of the queue, not of the data**: `REVISION_DAILY_CAP` is applied in `dueQueue()`, and both `/revision` and the dashboard card print how many more are past their date, because a page that quietly drops work is a page nobody trusts twice. **The migration backfills**, three per student per day, oldest solve first, at rung 0 — without it the feature is empty for exactly the students it exists for, and with a naive backfill those same students open it to a two-hundred-card wall. Surfaces: `/revision` (225 B of its own JS; the whole page is server-rendered), a dashboard card, and Revision second in the PRACTICE nav. Four CHECKs guard the card, because its columns are the only explanation a student ever gets for why a problem is in front of them today | PASS | Claude |
| 12c | 2026-10-30 | A bug the arithmetic hid, found by walking a real card up the ladder | **FIXED BEFORE SHIPPING.** The ledger row was first keyed on the rung the card came due on, which reads as the tightest possible key and is wrong: a card that lapses from rung 2 back to rung 1 must climb rung 1 again, and that rung's key was spent on the first climb. Because the ladder only advances when the row was actually inserted, the second climb would have paid nothing AND never moved — a student who forgot one problem once would have been stuck on it forever, shown the same card every day with no way to clear it. Nothing on paper showed this; it appeared the moment a DB test walked a card up, down one rung and up again. Both halves ship here, because a board nobody can moderate is not a feature at launch. **Two lists with different rules.** Solutions and tips need a solve to read and an UNASSISTED solve to post; questions need neither, because being stuck is the reason to ask. Only the ASKER and a mentor may reply to a question — the hole an anti-spoiler gate on solutions alone would leave open, since a question thread anybody could answer is the solution list again, reachable by exactly the people the gate keeps out. **One decider:** `discussionAccess()` in @recx/shared, reached only through `discussionAllowed()` on the help chokepoint (which now carries `assisted` beside `solved`), re-asked inside every action body. **Hidden, never deleted:** a moderator hides with a reason the author is shown word for word, un-hides, and marks the post worth reading first; nothing deletes, and the body of a hidden post is never fetched rather than fetched and filtered. Moderation is college-wide rather than per mentee — `mentor-scope` answers "whose student is this" for private data, and a queue narrowed to a mentor's own mentees would leave most of the board unmoderated while looking staffed. `/mentor/moderation` lists the open reports and, underneath, every post hidden in the last 30 days with who hid it, so a decision can be looked at by somebody who did not make it; `/mentor/doubts` lists every unanswered question, oldest first, and a thread leaves it when the ASKER marks an answer rather than when somebody replies. Splitting them means a student who needed a hint can still read and still ask, while the people posting are the people who got there alone, and the refusal says in as many words that it applies to this problem only and that using help was fine. The rungs: finish what you started; then a problem a company whose drive is inside 21 days has been reported asking (Phase 12e's tags); then a due revision (12c); then the topic with the fewest UNASSISTED solves against the PRODUCT per-topic target; then the plan, the daily challenge, anything. Two placements are arguments rather than conventions, and both are in the module header: FINISH is top because a problem already in the student's head is the cheapest thing they can finish tonight, and DRIVE is above REVISE because a drive has a date while a revision has a schedule built to tolerate a day. Rotation reads the topics of their last three judged PROBLEMS — one topic per problem, so five submissions on one array problem is one array problem — rather than our own past suggestions: same signal where it matters, and better where it differs, since a student who ignored three array suggestions has not done three array problems. Fenced by `tests/repo/recommender-posture.test.ts`: no ML package in any workspace manifest, the engine touches no database, clock or network, `recommend()` is called from exactly one place, and every rule the engine can return has an eyebrow and a call to action so none can render blank | PASS | Claude |

| 12k.1 | 2026-10-30 | Slice 1: the protocol goes to 4, and the SQL comparison is a pure module | **DONE.** `packages/shared/src/sql-problem.ts`: the dialect (PostgreSQL 17, stated rather than inferred), the limits the spec names (statement_timeout 5 s, one statement, 2,000 rows), the canonicalisation and the comparison. Two rules are arguments rather than conventions, and both are in the module header: a cell that LOOKS like a number is compared as a number (so `numeric`'s "1.50" and `float8`'s "1.5" are one answer, and a student is not failed for `sum(x)::int`), and column NAMES do not decide the verdict while the column COUNT does (so `count(*)` and `count(*) AS n` are one answer). NULL stays distinct from the empty string, because SQL's three-valued logic is the thing students get wrong. An ordered comparison that fails on rows that are nonetheless right reports ROW_ORDER — "the rows are right and the order is not" is one ORDER BY away, and is the most useful sentence available. Protocol 4 adds the engine discriminator, the SQL spec, and SQL's own five queues: `claimJob()` leases by id with no engine condition, so an isolate node would otherwise claim a SQL submission and refuse it, and three refusals fail a job | Claude |

| 12k.2 | 2026-10-30 | Slice 2: `apps/sql-judge`, the sandbox service | **DONE.** A judge node with a PostgreSQL sandbox instead of an isolate box: it consumes the SQL queues, claims through the same five endpoints, and posts the same result shape, so XP, the leaderboard, contests, assessments and the lease rules all keep meaning what they meant. Per case: the owner creates a schema, runs the author's setup and grants SELECT on what it made; the student's statement runs on its OWN connection as a role that owns nothing, inherits nothing and can create nothing, in a READ ONLY transaction with search_path pinned to that schema, statement_timeout at 5 s and a row cap; the schema is dropped in a `finally`, and a boot sweep drops anything a crash left behind. Two connections rather than `SET ROLE`, because one statement can call `set_config('role', …)` and a privileged session_user has something to go back to. One statement, because the EXTENDED protocol refuses a second command — the server's rule, not a semicolon count | Claude |
| 12k.2 | 2026-10-30 | A hang the first database run found, which no unit test could | **FIXED.** Every case whose statement ERRORED hung forever: the suite timed out on six of them at once. The cause is in node-postgres rather than in the SQL — a query executed with a row limit is a portal, and when such a query errors the client's queue can be left waiting, so the `ROLLBACK` in the cleanup never settles. An `await` on a promise that never settles is invisible to `catch`, which is why it read as a hang rather than a failure. The fix is that nothing is sent on that connection afterwards: a READ ONLY transaction has nothing to roll back, and closing the socket is what ends it. Closing now has a three-second deadline and destroys the socket if even that does not return, the schema drop has its own `lock_timeout`, and the boot sweep exists because this run had left six schemas behind — each one a dataset the student role still had SELECT on | Claude |

So the last calibration score of a dead node kept setting the threshold the live ones are measured against. On this workstation the seed holds three nodes and one was real; the two that had **never** heartbeated put the median 35.5% away and the page read *"calibration 368053000.0 deviates 35.5% from the fleet median 570776256.0 (limit 10.0%)"* with **ASSESSMENT / CONTEST LANES: Withheld**. The same shape in production is worse: half a fleet dying would bar the survivors from exams at exactly the wrong moment. Both places now take the median over nodes that are ONLINE **and** still heartbeating (`JUDGE_OFFLINE_AFTER_MS`), with a repository fence over both so the two cannot drift. Verified live: after the fix the word *deviates* is gone from `/admin/judge` and the only restriction left on that node is its own quarantine | Claude |

The SQL judge therefore booted, passed its adversarial cluster self-check, logged `sql judge ready` on `judge-sql-practice` / `judge-sql-run` — and every heartbeat came back `HTTP 401 — Judge node authentication failed.` as a *warning* in its own log, while a student's `sql17` submission sat at `QUEUED` / `DISPATCHED` for ever with nothing on the page to say why. Nobody reading the judge's log would have called it broken. The seed now reads a pair per engine (`DEV_JUDGE_NODES`) and registers both, naming any pair that is unset; `.env.example` offers `JUDGE_DEV_SQL_NODE_ID` / `_TOKEN`; the sandbox script's last line now tells the operator that registering is a second step, because that script is where the credentials are made and `apps/sql-judge` has no `.env.example` of its own. `tests/repo/dev-judge-nodes.test.ts` (5 tests) fences both halves — every pair the seed reads is documented, every pair documented is read, both default to the same node id — so a third engine cannot be added with half the wiring | Claude |

The scoring, ranking, freeze and rating code below it is all built and tested; what is missing is the one step that tells a submission which contest it belongs to. Needs a slice of its own, because it is a design decision and not a patch: how the arena carries the contest, what a practice submission made during a contest window means, which lane and caps apply, and what happens to a submission when the window closes mid-judge | Claude |

| 12q.3 | 2026-10-31 | Why `governance-invariants` fails in bursts: the WSL2 clock drifts DURING a run | **DIAGNOSED, not fixed.** The tests compare a PostgreSQL timestamp (the VM's clock) with a Node one (Windows'), and the file compensates with an offset measured once in `beforeAll`. | 12q.4 | 2026-10-31 | `governance-invariants` measured two clocks, and Prisma was the half nobody had looked at | **FIXED.** The file failed a different number of times each run (25, then 15, then none) on zero-tolerance constraints like `decidedAt >= requestedAt`, the failing row showing an "end" a hair before its "start". It already knew the clocks differed and compensated with an offset measured once in `beforeAll` — but a correction only holds while the thing it corrects for holds still, and WSL2's does not: measured across this session the database clock ran from **183 ms behind** Node to **403 ms ahead**, crossing zero in steps part way through a run. The half that was missing: **Prisma fills `@default(now())` in the CLIENT, not in the database** — measured here, a created row's timestamp tracked this process's clock to within 3 ms while sitting 327 ms from `now()`. So `requestedAt` and `startedAt` were Node timestamps all along, compared against a `decidedAt` / `endedAt` that raw SQL wrote as `now()`. The application never had the bug: the approve path writes `GREATEST(statement_timestamp(), "requestedAt")` for exactly this reason | Claude |
| 12q.4 | 2026-10-31 | How it is fixed, and how the scope was found | **One clock, the database's.** `dbNow()` asks PostgreSQL at the moment the answer is needed, and every column taking part in a zero-tolerance comparison is written from it — `requestedAt` and `expiresAt` on PrivilegedOperation, `requestedAt` and `validUntil` on Accommodation, `startedAt` and `expiresAt` on ImpersonationSession, and the `decidedAt`, `grantedAt`, `revokedAt` and `endedAt` beside them. The scope came from enumerating the **ten CHECKs that compare two timestamp columns** across the five tables this file covers (`pg_get_constraintdef` over `pg_constraint`), which is also how `ImpersonationSession_end_after_start_check` was caught — a session that could end before it began, and not one of the constraints that had been failing. AdminScope and Announcement have no such CHECK, so their timestamps were never at risk. The local clock keeps the two jobs it is right for, under a name that says so (`localMs`): measuring how long something took, and standing in for an application clock in the tests that probe the ±5-minute insert window, where a value deliberately off is the point | Claude |
| 12q.4 | 2026-10-31 | Two things the fix had to avoid, and a test that pins the cause | NOTED. Reading the clock from inside a `$transaction` would issue a query on another pooled connection while that one holds locks — in a file whose concurrency tests deliberately make connections wait for each other — so `approvalAt`, `baseAt` and `termsAt` are pure forms that take the instant, and the three in-transaction call sites read it before they open the transaction. And a new test asserts the mechanism itself: if a Prisma upgrade ever moves `@default(now())` into the database, it fails and the explicit writes can go. It skips itself when the two clocks agree, because there is then nothing to tell apart and nothing to go wrong | Claude |

The question and its **version** come from the attempt's own layout, never from the request, because a sitting is pinned to frozen versions so a problem republished mid-exam cannot change what is being answered. The snapshot is written **before** anything is queued, so the timeline a reviewer reads shows what was in the editor when it was run. A candidate sees "Question 4 of 9" whether 4 is a program or a question, so `position` is unique across **both** tables — which no single unique index can say. One guard function, two triggers told apart by `TG_TABLE_NAME`, and a db test from both directions. It raises `check_violation` rather than the `unique_violation` it actually is, because **Prisma maps `unique_violation` to its own P2002 and replaces the message with "Unique constraint failed on the (not available)"** — so the sentence explaining the clash never reaches the admin; found by a test expecting the wording and getting Prisma's. The options are shuffled per candidate and the answer is stored as **the position they clicked**, not a stored index: the other way round means trusting a browser to un-shuffle, and an out-of-range index would then be indistinguishable from a wrong answer rather than reported as corruption. Marking has four outcomes (correct / wrong / **blank** / **corrupt**), no negative marking, and the MCQs are laid out from their own per-question generator so adding one MCQ cannot silently re-order every candidate's coding questions — which would break the seed an appeal depends on, and has its own test | Claude |

So the value the client held was the one value no route would accept: every snapshot refused, every run refused, and every **submit** refused, so a candidate could not hand a paper in at all. Nothing looked wrong until a request was made, because the editor was the one consumer the value was right for, and both meanings are strings so no type could tell them apart. It is two fields now, named for what they are: `languageKey` goes to the routes, `editorLanguage` to the editor and nowhere else, with a fence that pins both — including the part that made it invisible, that `cpp20`'s editor mode is `"cpp"` | Claude |

The index a candidate clicks is a position in the order they were shown — the only index a browser is in a position to report honestly — and the save route checks it against the **layout**, not the request, so a question that is not on their paper is a 404 and an option they were never shown is a 400. The answer is the **line number** the author counted off the list they just typed: 1-based in the form, 0-based in the row, converted once at the boundary. Asking for the answer's text would mean matching strings, and a typo would be a question with no right answer. Two identical options are refused in the action, which the database cannot see. | 12s.1 | 2026-11-01 | Two per-paper options an invigilator can turn on, both asked for directly | DONE, both **off by default**, because the engine's standing decisions are the opposite ones and they are good ones. `refuseOutsidePaste`: a paste into the editor is refused unless the candidate copied that exact text inside this sitting. It is shaped so all three of SPEC §5's objections to blocking still hold — a candidate's own earlier work still pastes (the sitting already hashed everything copied inside it and classified each paste INTERNAL/EXTERNAL from that set), the refusal is still recorded as EXTERNAL_PASTE with its size so nothing is silenced, and only a paste INTO THE EDITOR is prevented, so copy, cut and the context menu are untouched (a test asserts there is exactly one `preventDefault` in the file). The decision is made synchronously from text held in the page, because `preventDefault` is only honoured inside the event's own tick; that text never leaves the browser. **Caught before shipping:** I had added a `refused` field to the proctor event — that schema is `.strict()` precisely so nothing can be added, so the server would have rejected the batch WHOLE and lost every event in it. `sitInPopup`: the sitting opens in a window of its own, sized to the screen, as an enhancement ON the readiness form rather than a replacement — so it still works before hydration, the window is opened inside the click, and a blocked popup falls back to the tab | Claude |

An **extension probe**: a page cannot enumerate extensions, and probing `web_accessible_resources` finds only extensions that publish a known resource under a known id — so it finds the off-the-shelf ones and reveals nothing about a purpose-built one, while the ones it does find reliably include screen readers, dyslexia fonts and colour filters. A signal whose best case is a casual cheat and whose common case is flagging a candidate for the software they need is not a security feature. And a **`DEVTOOLS_HEURISTIC` producer**, for the reason §7 already gives: a signal that fires because a student zoomed to 150 % to read the question is worse than no signal | Claude |
| 12s.2 | 2026-11-01 | Starting a sitting never checked containment — only the *next* request did | **FOUND AND FIXED**, by the route fence written in 12s.1: `checkContainment` runs on every request a sitting makes, through `guardAttempt`, but not on the request that CREATES the attempt. So a candidate who pressed Start from the wrong network, or in an ordinary browser instead of Safe Exam Browser, got a sitting: the row was written, the clock started, an attempt was spent, and the very next request refused it — on a paper with `maxAttempts: 1`, the whole paper. Containment was never what failed; what failed was charging a candidate to find out. Checked now before the attempt is created **and** before a resume (resuming spends nothing, but a candidate whose wifi moved should read the network's sentence rather than be let in and cut off a second later), with two new refusal messages that name the network or the .seb file. Also reordered: the paper-level facts (open, in its window, has questions, has an attempt left) are asked BEFORE containment, so a candidate whose paper is not open reads that rather than "only from the exam hall network" — both true, one of them the reason | Claude |
| 12s.2 | 2026-11-01 | The CIDR allowlist had never run once, on any request, for any sitting ever sat | **FOUND AND FIXED — pre-existing since Phase 6d, and the most serious defect of the phase.** `checkContainment` compares the candidate's address against the hall's blocks only when the attempt carries an `examSessionId` — correctly, since an unseated candidate has no hall to be inside — and **nothing in the application ever wrote that column**. Every attempt row carried null, so `docs/PROCTORING.md` §4's "re-verified on every heartbeat" was re-verification against nothing, and the invigilator's own roster (`where: { examSessionId }`) showed an empty room for every hall. The containment unit tests were green throughout because they pass `examSessionId: "sess_1"` as a fixture: they tested the function, which was right, and nothing tested whether anybody filled in what it reads. A sitting is now linked to its live exam session at start and seated on resume if it began before the hall existed, never re-pointed from one hall to another (a room whose allowlist admits anyone is the one thing the allowlist exists to prevent). `KIOSK_SEB` resolves a hall too, because the SEB keys rotate per exam session and a kiosk sitting with no hall could only ever be checked against the college-wide key | Claude |

| 12v.1 | 2026-11-01 | Every invigilator message ever sent was delivered to nobody | **FOUND AND FIXED — a five-phase hole in a shipped feature.** `messageCandidate` has existed since 6e: the desk says "Message sent.", the hall page counts what is unacknowledged, and the dialog promises the candidate "must acknowledge it before continuing". The heartbeat route answered with a hard-coded `pendingInvigilatorMessages: []`, under a comment reading "Phase 6e fills this from the invigilator desk" — which 6e did not. The contract was already right; only the two ends were missing. The heartbeat carries acknowledgements up (bounded, optional, on a `.strict()` request schema) and outstanding messages down; `applyHeartbeat` acknowledges **before** it reads, so a candidate who answers on one beat is not asked again on the same response. The write is scoped to the attempt rather than to the ids alone — an id is a cuid a client sends us — and is idempotent, because the client resends until the server stops calling the message pending. The candidate sees it in the focus overlay: opaque, focus-trapped, Escape refused, "I have read this" | Claude |

So `InactivityPolicy.enforceEnabled` (FALSE by default) decides it per policy version, and the fence is in `applyDecision()` — the only thing in the system that can restrict an account from a policy, because a check in the caller is a check the next caller forgets. `isActive: false` was not the answer either: a policy nobody configured is one the engine skips entirely, so it reports nothing. | 13g.1 | 2026-11-04 | **Every dialog in the application was unopenable in development** | FOUND AND FIXED. Clicking "Return to ONLINE" on `/admin/judge` did nothing at all -- no console error, no failed request, no React warning -- and the same was true of the language toggle beside it. A render probe showed the sequence: the state flipped to `online`, `Dialog` mounted with `open=true`, and the parent's state was back to `null` in the same frame. Two correct pieces working against each other: `Dialog` closes the native element in an unmount cleanup so focus returns to the control that opened it, and it listens for the element's native `close` event because a browser can close a modal on its own; React remounts every effect once on mount in development, which ran that cleanup while the dialog was still mounted and open, and the queued `close` event was read as the user dismissing it. It reached all 23 dialogs, several of them behind SECURITY-audited operations -- returning a judge node to service, enabling a language, a legal hold, a purge. The fix is a flag the component raises while it closes the element itself, checked before the handler decides anything | Claude |
| 13g.1 | 2026-11-04 | Why no test had caught it | NOTED. The end-to-end specs that drive a dialog (`authoring-journey`'s retirement, `judge-resilience`) run against a production build, where React does not remount effects, so the bug was invisible to every suite that could have seen it; and there is no DOM test environment in this repository, so no unit test could render the component's effects at all. The gate is where it was missing, which is where the guard went | Claude |
| 13g.1 | 2026-11-04 | `tests/repo/dialog-self-close.test.ts` | ADDED, 3 tests. Pins the shape of the fix over the 40 lines that hold it: exactly one `close()` in the file and it belongs to the helper that raises the flag first, the native handler checks the flag before every other decision and lowers it again so a real browser close is still heard, and the unmount cleanup that made the flag necessary is still there -- a "fix" that deleted the cleanup would pass the first two checks and silently lose the focus return | Claude |

| 13i.1 | 2026-11-05 | What each remedy does, and what was deliberately left out | NOTED. FULL_MARKS gives every candidate the question's marks whatever they answered, blank papers included, and the paper is still worth the same total. DROPPED takes the question out of every score AND out of the total -- a paper of 50 becomes a paper of 47 -- because a withdrawn question left in the denominator makes every percentage on the page wrong. Both decide BEFORE the ordinary marking, so a dropped question cannot still be reported as pending, wrong or corrupt, and cannot keep a result provisional. A third remedy that changes the correct answer and re-marks is deliberately absent: it makes one candidate's wrong answer right and another's right answer wrong, and that belongs to editing the question and rejudging, where the history is kept | Claude |

| 14l | 2026-11-07 | The range parser is narrow on purpose | DONE, and pure, so the awkward cases are tested rather than reasoned about: a suffix range is the LAST n bytes, an end past the file is clamped rather than refused, a start past the end is unsatisfiable, `bytes=-0` is unsatisfiable, an unknown unit is ignored as RFC 9110 requires, and a multi-range request is answered with the whole file rather than with a multipart body nobody here would otherwise write or test. Sixteen new tests | Claude |

Next answers a HEAD by running the GET and dropping the body, which on this route means streaming 116 MB off the disk so it can be discarded. | 14l | 2026-11-07 | Resumes are counted separately, and the trade is written down | DONE. One resumed download is several requests and a download manager opens a handful at once, so charging those against the twenty full-download attempts would cut off exactly the candidate the feature is for: 120 per ten minutes for a resume, 20 for a whole download. `bytes=0-` is a whole download wearing a range header and is charged to the strict limit.
